- fix: While lock.locked: in the Back callback, do nothing except let the system leave the app; in LockScreen call LocalFocusManager.clearFocus(force = true) on entry and make the overlay a focus trap (focusable + onPreviewKeyEvent { true }), or simpler and stronger: do not compose AppShell at all while locked on devices where blur is not supported, and on blur devices render it from a captured layer.
- second reviewer: MainActivity.kt:43-44 calls shell.handleBack() with no lock check, and AppShell.kt:78 / Overlay.kt:54-57 then run the top overlay's onBack or pop a screen; a repo-wide search finds no BackHandler, key trap or clearFocus tied to the lock (LockScreen.kt:70 is touch-only, MasroufyApp.kt:101 only clears semantics). Back-key part is certain from the code; the hardware-keyboard part remains unproven (not tested on a device). No data is exposed, only blind state changes, so low is fair.

### device-lock-3 - The app lock is a screen gate only: phone PIN opens it, no key is bound to it, and everything on disk stays readable
- severity: low | verdict: weakened | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/AndroidDeviceLock.kt:35-45 and :52-56; native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/AndroidLocalFiles.kt:102-109`
- evidence: There is no app PIN at all (nothing stored, so no hash/salt question). AndroidDeviceLock.kt:42-45 calls prompt.authenticate(info.build()) with no CryptoObject; success is just the onAuthenticationSucceeded callback at :36 turning into LockResult.OK. Allowed authenticators (:52-56) always include DEVICE_CREDENTIAL, and on Android 9/10 they fall back to BIOMETRIC_WEAK (2D face unlock class). The 'lock enabled' flag is a plain boolean in the 'app-lock' SharedPreferences file (AndroidLocalFiles.kt:103-108). Attempt limits and lockout are the system's own (the app adds none, which is correct for this design).
- impact: 1) Anyone who knows the phone's PIN/pattern (family member) opens the app - the lock adds nothing over the phone lock for that person. 2) On a rooted phone or with a hooking tool the callback or the boolean can be flipped, and the local data (Firestore offline copy, SMS inbox database, repair backups, problem log) is not encrypted by the app, only by Android's own storage encryption. For a personal-finance app on a non-rooted phone this is an acceptable baseline, but it should be a recorded decision, not an accident.
- fix: Minimum: record in OVERRIDES that the lock is 'phone lock re-prompt, not encryption'. Optional hardening: offer 'fingerprint only' (BIOMETRIC_STRONG without DEVICE_CREDENTIAL) as a setting; if real protection at rest is ever wanted, bind an AndroidKeyStore key (setUserAuthenticationRequired) through a CryptoObject and use it to encrypt the SMS inbox database and backups.
- second reviewer: Technical facts hold (AndroidDeviceLock.kt:42-45 no CryptoObject, :52-56 DEVICE_CREDENTIAL always allowed and BIOMETRIC_WEAK below Android 11; AndroidLocalFiles.kt:102-109 plain boolean). But the 'phone PIN opens it' half is not an accident: it is a recorded owner decision in OVERRIDES.md:307-311 (section 21, 2026-09-13), so only the 'no encryption at rest / root can flip it' half is unrecorded. That remainder is informational for a non-rooted personal phone; the owner question about PIN fallback is already answered and should not be re-asked as open.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: Is it acceptable that anyone who knows the phone's PIN can open the app, or do you want a 'fingerprint only' option?

### device-lock-4 - Removing the phone's screen lock silently and permanently turns the app lock off (and screenshot blocking with it)
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/app/src/commonMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/usecase/AppLock.kt:64-73; native-app/ui/src/commonMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/ui/app/LockGate.kt:48-54; native-app/androidApp/src/main/kotlin/app/masroufy/android/MainActivity.kt:122-127`
- evidence: AppLock.releaseIfDeviceHasNoLock (68-73) writes settings.write(false) when availability is NONE_ENROLLED; LockGate.unlock (50-54) then sets locked = false and shows a one-time card. The code comment at AppLock.kt:66 marks this as provisional behaviour awaiting the owner. Because hideWhenLocked (MainActivity.kt:122-127) ties FLAG_SECURE to the same flag, screenshots and the recents thumbnail become visible again too. Nothing re-enables the lock when a phone lock is set again.
- impact: Removing the phone lock needs the current phone credential, so this is not a bypass for a stranger. The risk is the owner (or a family member who knows the PIN) removing the phone lock once: the app then stays unlocked for good, even after a new phone lock is set, and the owner may not notice beyond one card.
- fix: Do not write false. Keep the setting on, let the user in for this session with the clear card, and lock again normally as soon as the device has a lock (availability becomes SUCCESS). Alternatively keep the app closed with a message 'set a phone lock to open'.
- second reviewer: AppLock.kt:68-73 writes settings.write(false) on NONE_ENROLLED and nothing re-enables it; LockGate.kt:50-54 then unlocks with a one-time card, and MainActivity.kt:122-127 ties FLAG_SECURE and the recents thumbnail to the same flag, so both drop too. Not a bypass for a stranger (removing the phone lock needs the credential) and the code itself marks it as provisional awaiting the owner (AppLock.kt:66), so low with an owner decision is the right framing.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: When the phone has no screen lock at all: open the app with a warning and re-lock later (recommended), or keep it closed until a phone lock is set?

### device-lock-5 - Waiting bank-SMS texts and 'before repair' backups sit in plain files with no age limit
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/SmsInboxStore.kt:22, :172-182, :186-187, :206-213; native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/AndroidLocalFiles.kt:25-41`
- evidence: SmsInboxStore.kt:22 creates a plain SQLite table with a body TEXT column; enqueue (172-182) stores the sanitized body; the body is blanked only in acknowledge (206-213) or on sign-out (forget, :66-69). The comment at :186-187 says thousands of rows can be waiting after a history read. I found no time-based purge of rows with done=0 (lines 70-107 of the file were not read). AndroidLocalFiles.kt:25-41 writes the full 'before repair' account copy as plain text into noBackupFilesDir/backups and keeps the last two. Good points already in place: SmsSafety.sanitize (device/src/commonMain/.../SmsSafety.kt:63-73) drops OTP messages and masks non-money numbers before storage, the folder is app-private and excluded from backup, and both are wiped at sign-out.
- impact: Only reachable with root, a forensic image, or a lost phone whose storage encryption is defeated. In that case amounts, merchants, dates and balances from unconfirmed messages (possibly months old) and two full account copies are readable without the app lock.
- fix: Add an age cap for waiting rows (for example blank the body after 90 days, keep the id so it is not re-read) and state the cap in OVERRIDES. If device-lock-3's key-bound encryption is ever done, apply it to these two stores first.
- second reviewer: Read the whole SmsInboxStore.kt including the previously unread lines 70-107 (only cursor and per-sender settings): the only writes to the messages table are delete on sign-out (:68), insert (:182) and blank-on-acknowledge (:214), so there is no age-based purge of waiting rows. AndroidLocalFiles.kt:25-41 writes backups as plain text, last two kept. Mitigations hold and one is stronger than stated: AndroidManifest.xml:22 sets allowBackup=false, so the SMS database (not only the backups folder) is out of Android backup. Reachable only with root or broken device encryption; low, arguably informational.

## attack-surface

- not checked: Read-only review, 37 tool calls, nothing built or run. Checked and found clean (no finding): main app manifest has one exported component, the launcher activity, with no deep-link/VIEW filters, no providers, no FileProvider, no services, no taskAffinity/launchMode overrides (androidApp/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml:39-48); the SMS receiver is exported but protected by BROADCAST_SMS and checks the action (device manifest line 9, BankSmsReceiver.kt:17); the only PendingIntent is FLAG_IMMUTABLE (AndroidDeviceNotifier.kt:118); the `perf_uid` launch extra read on the exported activity (MainActivity.kt:52) is neutralised outside emulator builds by a build-time constant (AppContainer.kt:142); the other launch extra only opens the bank-messages screen; no WebView, no ACTION_VIEW on data-derived URLs, and no network client other than the fixed-URL feed fetch were found, so the assistant's free text does not appear to leave the device (grep-based, not a full trace). SMS bodies are capped at 8000 characters before any parsing (SmsSafety.kt:64) and CSV import caps rows at 100,000 (CsvReader.kt:21,42).\n\nNot checked: (1) catastrophic backtracking across the ~387 regexes in core (only SmsSafety.numbers, SHAPE_DOUBT, DATE_TOKEN and a pattern grep for nested quantifiers were looked at; nothing obviously exponential, but the Saudi/Egypt shape tables, SmsSaudiLines, SmsTransferParties, AlrajhiPdf and QnbPdf regexes were not read, and PDF/CSV text is not length-capped per cell the way SMS is); (2) smsLegVerdict / SmsAccountLegs, SmsSalaryEffect, OwnAccountByLast4Effect and rule creation on the auto-record path, so whether a forged message can create own-account transfer legs, an auto-tagged salary, or a categorisation rule is unverified; (3) ReviewSmsInbox.recordLocked and ImportStatement internals (what `verified = true` and `clearOnly = true` exactly permit); (4) the manual 'read bank SMS' path (AndroidBankSms.kt, ReadBankSms.kt); (5) backup restore parsing of a crafted JSON (BackupCheck.kt) beyond its 50 MB bounded read; (6) CsvReader cell/line length limits and path handling in AndroidLocalFiles / AndroidPersonPhotos (image decode is sample-sized, not examined further); (7) which columns ExportCsv writes; (8) LoadOnlineFeeds URL and JSON parsing; (9) BackgroundWork scheduling abuse (SMS flood triggering repeated runs); (10) the emulator network-security XML, the iOS targets, the firebase-trial test app beyond its manifest (cleartext + exported launcher, stated as not distributed), and the instrumented-test manifests.

### attack-surface-1 - Bank SMS trust is a sender-name string match only; a spoofed sender ID is auto-recorded silently once a layout is learned
- severity: low (finder said medium) | verdict: weakened | urgency: before_release
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/SmsInboxStore.kt:42 and :172-183; BankSmsReceiver.kt:40-44; AndroidSmsInbox.kt:141-158; native-app/app/src/commonMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/usecase/AutoRecordSms.kt:178-180; SmsRouting.kt:45-48 and :88-91`
- evidence: accepts() is a case-insensitive equality between the SMS originating address and the owner's enabled sender names; there is no other origin check (the receiver itself is correctly protected by BROADCAST_SMS, device manifest line 9). The inbox catch-up selects rows by `address = ? COLLATE NOCASE` only. AutoRecordSms.walk then calls recordLocked(emptyMap(), emptyList(), clearOnly = true, verified = true) with no owner choice, and the class comment (line 25) states auto-recorded messages produce no notification. The only gates are content-based: learned layout fingerprint (SmsLearning.kt:59), SHAPE_DOUBT words, single date, date window (SmsShapeGate.kt:115-122), and wait reasons for refund / other own account / cash (SmsWaitReasons.kt:55-65). When the message carries no matching account digits, legWallet falls back to the sender's wallet (the mapped one, or the only bank wallet in the country).
- impact: Alphanumeric sender IDs are forgeable through bulk SMS gateways in many markets. Anyone who knows the owner's bank and copies a real message layout (layouts are public and the fingerprint ignores amount, merchant, date, card digits) can inject purchases or incoming transfers that become real transactions and shift the wallet balance, with no prompt and no notification. No money moves, but balances, budgets and the salary/income picture become wrong and the owner has no signal to look. Not verified: whether a purchase message with card digits that match no wallet is held (smsLegVerdict was not read), and whether a spoofed salary message is auto-tagged after the owner answered the one-time salary question for that sender.
- fix: Treat auto-recording as untrusted-by-origin: (1) require the message's own-account/card last-4 to match the target wallet before auto-recording, otherwise wait; (2) add an auto-record ceiling (per message amount and per day count) above which the message waits for confirmation; (3) show a quiet daily digest of what was auto-recorded so injected items are visible; (4) hold incoming-money messages (credits, salary) for confirmation unless they match an expected source.
- second reviewer: Code is as described: SmsInboxStore.kt:42 and :173 match the sender name only, the receiver is guarded by BROADCAST_SMS (device AndroidManifest.xml:9), and AutoRecordSms.kt:180 records with no owner choice. The open question resolves against the app: SmsRouting.kt:88-91 falls back to the sender's wallet when digits match nothing, and SmsWaitReasons.kt:42-45 and :57 only hold digits belonging to another own wallet or an unknown from/to transfer leg; no amount ceiling or digest of auto-recorded items was found. Severity lowered because it needs spoofing a registered bank sender ID at one specific phone, plus an already-learned layout, for no gain beyond corrupting a private ledger whose entries stay visible and deletable; still worth the owner decision.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: Should auto-recorded bank messages be limited by amount / require matching account digits, and should the owner get a daily summary of what was recorded without asking?

### attack-surface-2 - The 'arrival day' used by the SMS date gates comes from the sender-supplied timestamp, not the device clock
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/BankSmsReceiver.kt:44; AndroidSmsInbox.kt:156-158; native-app/core/src/commonMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/core/SmsShapeGate.kt:115-133`
- evidence: The receiver stores parts[0].timestampMillis (the service-centre timestamp carried inside the message) as the received time; the catch-up path prefers the `date_sent` column over the device `date` column (`if (sent > 0) sent else date`). gateShape compares the transaction date in the body against arrivalDay to block future-dated and older-than-60-days messages. I did not trace the exact conversion from the stored timestamp to arrivalDay, but the parser tests pass receivedAt on the message object as the only time input.
- impact: A forged message can carry a back-dated service-centre timestamp together with a matching back-dated date in its body, so the 60-day window and the 'date after arrival' gate both pass. That lets an injected message land in an old, already-reviewed financial month where it is least likely to be noticed. Only meaningful in combination with attack-surface-1.
- fix: Keep the sent timestamp for the dedupe key (so receiver and catch-up stay consistent), but compute the gate's arrival day from the device receive time (the inbox `date` column / System.currentTimeMillis in the receiver), and make a large gap between sent time and device receive time a reason to wait.
- second reviewer: BankSmsReceiver.kt:44 stores the service-centre timestamp and AndroidSmsInbox.kt:158 prefers date_sent; that value becomes receivedAt (SmsInboxStore.kt:196) and the gate's arrival day is derived from it (BankSmsParser.kt:230, EgyptBankSms.kt:134, SmsShapeGate.kt:118-119). So the 60-day and future-date gates trust a sender-supplied time; only matters together with attack-surface-1, and the dedupe key also depends on this timestamp so the fix must keep it for the key.

### attack-surface-3 - Statement file picker reads the whole file into memory when the provider does not report a size
- severity: low | verdict: weakened | urgency: before_release
- where: `native-app/ui/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/ui/screens/imports/FilePicker.android.kt:37-49 (contrast native-app/ui/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/ui/screens/more/BackupFiles.android.kt:86-98)`
- evidence: size starts at -1 and is only set if the provider returns OpenableColumns.SIZE; the 25 MB check `if (size > MAX_FILE_BYTES)` is skipped when size is unknown, then `openInputStream(uri)?.use { it.readBytes() }` reads everything and only afterwards compares bytes.size to the limit. The backup picker in the same module already does the right thing: a bounded loop that rejects as soon as the total passes the limit.
- impact: Cloud and third-party document providers often omit the size. Picking a very large or endless stream (a huge file in Drive, or a hostile provider app) exhausts memory and crashes the app during import. Local denial of service only; needs the owner to pick the file.
- fix: Reuse the bounded read loop from BackupFiles.android.kt (read up to MAX_FILE_BYTES + 1, reject on overflow) in the statement picker.
- second reviewer: FilePicker.android.kt:47-49 does skip the size check when the provider reports no size and reads everything before comparing to the 25 MB limit (FilePicker.kt:9), while BackupFiles.android.kt:86-98 has the bounded loop. But the read is inside Kotlin runCatching (line 48), which catches Throwable including OutOfMemoryError, so the likely result is a silently empty file rather than a crash; still a cheap fix, urgency can be 'later'.

### attack-surface-4 - PDF import has no page-count, object-size or time limit before parsing with PdfBox
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/PdfBoxPages.kt:28-48`
- evidence: PDDocument.load(data) is called on the raw bytes with no memory setting, then `(1..pdf.numberOfPages).map` runs a text stripper per page and keeps every TextPosition of every page in lists. The only bound is the 25 MB input size from the picker (and that is bypassable, see attack-surface-3). No cap on numberOfPages, on collected characters, and no timeout or cancellation check.
- impact: A small crafted PDF (highly compressed streams, or thousands of pages / millions of glyphs) expands far beyond 25 MB in memory and can freeze or crash the import. The owner would have to pick such a file, e.g. a fake 'statement' received by email or chat. Denial of service only; no data leaves the device.
- fix: Reject documents above a sane page count (for example 500), stop collecting once a character budget per document is exceeded, load with a temp-file-backed memory setting instead of main memory only, and make the loop cancellable so leaving the screen stops the work.
- second reviewer: PdfBoxPages.kt:31 loads with default main-memory settings and :40-47 loops over every page with no page or character cap and no cancellation point (onProgress is not suspending); ReadPdfStatement.kt:44-45 adds no limit upstream. Self-inflicted denial of service only: the owner must pick a crafted file and the worst case is a frozen or crashed import.

### attack-surface-5 - CSV export does not neutralise spreadsheet formulas in text that originates from SMS or imported statements
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/app/src/commonMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/usecase/ExportCsv.kt:23 and :43`
- evidence: escape() only adds quotes when the value contains a quote, comma or line break (`NEEDS_QUOTES = Regex("[\",\n\r]")`); a search of the file for any handling of leading =, +, -, @ found nothing. I did not read which columns are written, so whether merchant name / description / note are among them is assumed from the purpose of the export, not confirmed.
- impact: A merchant or party name that begins with = + - or @ (which can arrive from a bank message, an imported statement row, or the shared merchant catalogue covered in sec-firebase-1) is written as-is. When the owner or an accountant opens the export in Excel or Sheets the cell is evaluated as a formula, which can show misleading numbers or pull an external link. Requires the hostile text to get into the data first.
- fix: In escape(), prefix any text cell that starts with =, +, -, @, tab or carriage return with a single quote (and always quote it); leave numeric amount columns untouched.
- second reviewer: ExportCsv.kt:43 only quotes on quote, comma or line break, and :57 writes rawMerchantName or rawDescription (text from SMS or statements) into the name column, so the unconfirmed column assumption holds. Two corrections: the exported name is the raw text, not the catalogue display name, so the shared-catalogue route does not apply; and the file is meant to be re-importable (line 12), so a quote prefix must be stripped on import or the round trip changes names.

### attack-surface-6 - Online feed download reads the full response body with no size cap
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `native-app/device/src/androidMain/kotlin/app/masroufy/device/PlatformHttpText.android.kt:35`
- evidence: `connection.inputStream.use { it.readBytes().toString(Charsets.UTF_8) }` with connect/read timeouts but no Content-Length check and no maximum. The header comment says the URL is fixed in LoadOnlineFeeds and https only (I did not open LoadOnlineFeeds to confirm the host). This is a different angle from sec-firebase-4, which covers value plausibility, not size.
- impact: If the feed file in the public repo is replaced by a very large one (compromised repo, bad workflow run), every client that refreshes prices allocates the whole body and may crash at start-up or during background refresh. Low likelihood, wide blast radius.
- fix: Read through a bounded loop (for example 2 MB) and fail with HttpFailure when exceeded; also reject when Content-Length is above the cap before reading.
- second reviewer: PlatformHttpText.android.kt:35 reads the whole body with no cap, and only IOException is caught there, so an out-of-memory error would propagate. The URL is a fixed https constant pointing at the owner's own GitHub repo (LoadOnlineFeeds.kt:31, :71), and the body is parsed before being cached (:72-76), so this needs a repo compromise or a broken workflow run; likelihood very low, fix is a few lines.

## repo-exposure

- not checked: Checked and found clean (no finding): (1) Secrets in history across all refs for private-key markers, service-account fields, GitHub/Slack/Anthropic token prefixes, FIREBASE_TOKEN - no hits; storePassword/keyPassword hits (commit d7c39391, android/app/build.gradle:23,25) are the standard Android debug keystore values with alias androiddebugkey, and the keystore file itself is outside the repo; the AIza hit in FIREBASE.md (commit e8c06bd5) is the public web config already covered by sec-firebase-2. (2) No google-services.json, keystore, APK/AAB, PDF or real CSV ever added on any ref; the only zip is the design package (29 text files). (3) Workflows: no pull_request or pull_request_target trigger anywhere, so a fork PR cannot run code with secrets or write access; prices.yml has top-level permissions {} with contents: write on one job, actions pinned by hash, persist-credentials false, no third-party npm install, and writes only public/prices.json and public/averages.json; native-core.yml is read-only with no secrets (unpinned tags already reported as sec-firebase-10). (4) Test SMS data: the single IBAN-like value repeated in golden/sms.json and tests is the well-known published example IBAN; phone-like values are overwhelmingly placeholder patterns; the one 9665 hit in golden/pdf.json is part of a coordinate decimal, not a phone number. FirebaseSetup.kt only holds emulator placeholder values.

NOT checked: GitHub-side settings (branch protection on main, default Actions token permissions, who can trigger workflow_dispatch, secret scanning / push protection, whether Actions from forks need approval, existing repo secrets) - not visible from a clone; the direct bot push to main suggests main has no required-review rule but I could not confirm. The private feedback repo and whether the template was ever uploaded there. Whether the web.app site is actually still serving (no network requests made) and its live response headers. Contents of the 86 PNG/JPG files (only their folders: app icons and bank logos) and of screenshots outside git. Unreachable/dangling commits and GitHub-cached views of force-pushed history; stashes. Line-by-line reading of HANDOVER.md / HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md / OVERRIDES.md for real people's names, tester identities or transfer counterparties - only pattern searches (IBAN, phone, email, uid, fingerprints, credentials) were run; three 'password:'-style pattern hits in ARCHITECTURE.md, HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md and OVERRIDES.md were counted but not opened. Whether the 2 Saudi-format and ~15 Egyptian-format phone-like strings in SMS test cases and research/banks (sourced from third-party open datasets of real SMS) are fully invented. rule-reference.json contents (only its README description was read). The other two remote branches were covered only by the history-wide path and marker searches, not browsed. No build, gradle, emulator or edit was run.

### repo-exposure-1 - The owner's real financial profile is readable in the public repo (bank, balances with dates, 21-month totals, salary day, real merchant list)
- severity: medium | verdict: confirmed | urgency: before_release
- where: `CLAUDE.md:26,72-74,84,89 · native-app/core/src/jvmTest/kotlin/app/masroufy/core/RealStatementTest.kt:21,28-31,40-42 · design-source/masroofi-claude-code/fixtures/merchant-reference.json (319 names) · design-source/masroofi-claude-code/legacy/rules_categories.csv · masroofi-claude-code.zip`
- evidence: The raw statement itself is NOT in the repo or its history (files/ is ignored at .gitignore:13; a history scan of every ref for added pdf/csv/xlsx/zip/apk/keystore paths found only synthetic fixtures). But the derived facts are: CLAUDE.md:72-74 gives the named bank, the exact opening balance and date, the cash wallet amount and date, and the salary day; CLAUDE.md:84-89 gives total debit, total credit, real spend and the closing balance and date. RealStatementTest.kt:21 adds the row count (1912) and lines 28-43 repeat the totals. The same numbers appear in 25 tracked files (SPEC.md, OVERRIDES.md, ARCHITECTURE.md, START_HERE.md, KICKOFF.txt, tests/acceptance/realStatement.test.ts, etc.). design-source/.../fixtures/README.md states merchant-reference.json and rule-reference.json are 'names/categories from the previous work files', and legacy/README.md says rules_categories.csv was 'copied as is from the user's files' - i.e. the real list of places the owner pays (travel, government services, telecom, insurance, wallets). reference/history-v3-ar.md:39 adds the average monthly spend. The owner's first name (CLAUDE.md:26), GitHub handle and personal email (see repo-exposure-2) tie this to one identifiable person.
- impact: Anyone on the internet can read one named person's bank, approximate income and spending level, account balance on given dates, payday, and the shops and services he uses. This is useful for targeted phishing ('your account at <bank>, balance X') and is the kind of data the app itself promises never to upload. It stays in git history even if the files are edited later.
- fix: Decide whether the repo should stay public. If yes: replace the real totals in docs/tests with a reference to a local-only file (the real-statement tests already read the CSV path from a system property, so the expected totals can come from an ignored local file too), strip or synthesize merchant-reference.json / rule-reference.json / rules_categories.csv, drop masroofi-claude-code.zip (it duplicates the same files), and accept that old history still contains them unless history is rewritten or the repo is made private.
- second reviewer: Checked CLAUDE.md:72-74,84-89 (4 of the reference figures present), RealStatementTest.kt:21,28-42 (totals, opening/closing balance and dates hard-coded although the CSV path is a system property at :15), fixtures/README.md:9 and legacy/README.md:3 (both state the merchant/rule lists come from the owner's previous work files), merchant-reference.json (319 entries; one name carries an 11-digit run worth a look), and the tracked zip (29 files, duplicates both lists). All four are on origin/main; git grep finds the figures in 29 tracked files (finding said 25). No raw statement, no full account numbers in these files. Extra point: HANDOVER.md:58 states the repo is public and that no merchant name or amount from the owner's files is in it, so this contradicts the project's own rule. Medium is fair: aged balances and a shop list, not transactions, but attributable to one named person.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: Keep the repo public with these real figures and merchant names visible, scrub them going forward (history still shows them), or make the repo private (note: the price feed is read from raw.githubusercontent.com on main and the free macOS runner depends on the repo being public).

### repo-exposure-2 - Personal email, Windows user name and local folder layout are published in commits and docs
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `git history (author field) · FIREBASE.md:14 · START_HERE.md:59 · .claude/launch.json:13 · HANDOVER.md (3 lines) · HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md (5 lines, plus :2228)`
- evidence: git log --all --format=%ae: 1,378 commits carry one personal gmail address as author (421 of them on origin/main), plus 2 commits with a second gmail address. The same address is written in FIREBASE.md:14 (support email row) and START_HERE.md:59 (git config command). .claude/launch.json:13 is tracked and contains a C:/Users/<windows-user>/AppData/... scratchpad path; HANDOVER.md and HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md contain 8 more C:\Users\<user> paths. HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md:2228 prints the SHA-1 fingerprint of the debug signing key. A removed file native-app/build.log (commits 55957e19..0272dacb) shows the absolute path of the local secrets folder.
- impact: Low on its own: gives a stranger the owner's email for phishing/spam, his machine user name and where secrets and keystores live on disk. It makes finding 1 attributable to a real person. The SHA-1 fingerprint is not secret but confirms which key signs every distributed build.
- fix: Set git user.email to the GitHub noreply address for future commits; replace the address in START_HERE.md and use a dedicated support mailbox in FIREBASE.md; untrack .claude/launch.json (add .claude/ to .gitignore); avoid absolute user paths in handover notes.
- second reviewer: FIREBASE.md:14 and START_HERE.md:59 each hold one personal gmail address; .claude/launch.json is tracked and has 1 C:/Users path, HANDOVER.md 3, HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md 5; one SHA-1 fingerprint line exists in HANDOVER-ARCHIVE.md; native-app/build.log at 55957e19 mentions the secrets folder path. Author counts differ slightly from the finding: 1380 commits with gmail authors and 81 with noreply, 4 distinct addresses in total. Low is right: author emails in commits are normal for any public repo and none of this is a credential.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: Whether to switch to a noreply commit email and a separate support address.

### repo-exposure-3 - .gitignore does not cover the service-account key the feedback README tells the owner to download, nor several other risky file types
- severity: medium | verdict: confirmed | urgency: now
- where: `.gitignore:13,40,46,59-60 · tools/feedback-action/README.md:17-36`
- evidence: git check-ignore probes (no files created): NOT ignored - serviceAccount.json, <project>-firebase-adminsdk-*.json (the exact default name of the file from 'Generate new private key', which tools/feedback-action/README.md step 1 instructs the owner to download), native-app/keystore.properties, native-app/signing.properties, native-app/androidApp/*.log and native-app/firebase-trial/*.log (only native-app/*.log at root is ignored, line 60), native-app/firestore/emulator/firebase-export/*.json, *.zip, *.xlsx, *.csv outside files/, *.hprof. Ignored correctly: local.properties, google-services.json anywhere, *.apk/*.aab, *.jks/*.keystore/*.p12, .env*. scripts/secretscan.mjs:35-39 flags by file name only google-services, .env, keystores and zip/apk, and per sec-firebase-5 it is run by hand. native-app/build.log was already committed once (4 commits) before the log rule was added; a results .txt file is still tracked.
- impact: The admin key bypasses every Firestore rule and can read or delete all users' data. With an AI agent committing with broad 'git add', one download into the project folder is enough to publish it; on a public repo that is harvested within minutes and cannot be undone by deleting the commit. Same for a future release keystore password file or an emulator export / spreadsheet holding real data.
- fix: Add to the root .gitignore: *firebase-adminsdk*.json, *service-account*.json, serviceAccount*.json, keystore.properties, signing.properties, **/*.log, **/firebase-export/, *.hprof, and (with explicit exceptions for the fixtures) *.xlsx, *.xls, /*.zip. Add the same file-name patterns plus the private-key marker to secretscan. In the README, tell the owner to save the key outside the project folder.
- second reviewer: Re-ran the check-ignore probes against .gitignore:1-60 and native-app/.gitignore:1-6: serviceAccount.json, *-firebase-adminsdk-*.json, native-app/keystore.properties, signing.properties, nested *.log, firebase-export json, zip/xlsx/csv/hprof are all NOT ignored. One correction: root local.properties is also NOT ignored (only android/ and native-app/ copies are), contrary to the finding. scripts/secretscan.mjs:35-41 name list has no service-account pattern, and I found no git hook, package.json script or workflow that runs it; the tracked zip also violates its own zip rule. Likelihood is lower than stated: README.md:25 and :36 already tell the owner never to put the key in a repo and to delete it, and a browser download lands outside the project by default. Keep medium because the fix is a few ignore lines and the blast radius is full admin access on a public repo; urgency 'now' is better read as 'before the owner does README step 1'.

### repo-exposure-4 - Feedback workflow template feeds the admin key to unpinned code and posts user text into issues unfiltered
- severity: low | verdict: weakened | urgency: before_release
- where: `tools/feedback-action/feedback.yml:13-14,25,27,32,36 · tools/feedback-action/inbox-to-issues.mjs:58,66-67,93`
- evidence: feedback.yml:25 and :27 use actions/checkout@v4 and actions/setup-node@v4 by moving tag (prices.yml pins both to full commit hashes; this template was not given the same treatment). :32 installs firebase-admin@12.7.0 at run time with no lockfile, so its ~100 transitive packages float; the next step (:36) loads that code with FIREBASE_SA_JSON in the environment and a token with contents: write + issues: write (:13-14). The key is the default Admin SDK key (README step 1), i.e. full project access, not a role limited to the inbox collection. inbox-to-issues.mjs:66-67 builds the issue title and body from the raw inbox text written by any signed-in app user: no neutralising of @mentions, links or HTML. :93 reads every unconfirmed sharedMerchants document with no limit on each 3-hourly run.
- impact: A compromised action tag or npm dependency gets full read/write over every user's financial data. A stranger with a free account can make the owner's bot ping arbitrary GitHub users (@name) and plant phishing links in the owner's issue tracker; flooding sharedMerchants (already noted in sec-firebase-1) also burns the daily read quota from this job. The template is described as not yet uploaded, so this is a fix-before-use item.
- fix: Pin both actions to commit hashes; commit a package-lock.json in the private repo and use npm ci --ignore-scripts; create a dedicated service account with only Datastore access and rotate it periodically; wrap user text in a code block or insert a zero-width space after '@' and cap its length; add .limit() to the sharedMerchants query.
- second reviewer: Code is as described: feedback.yml:25,27 use moving tags (prices.yml:37,42 pin by hash), :32 installs firebase-admin without a lockfile, :13-14 grant contents+issues write; inbox-to-issues.mjs:66-67 uses raw text, :93 has no limit. But the impact is overstated: the target repo is private (feedback.yml:2, README.md:3), so @mentions of people without access do not notify them and any planted link is seen only by the owner; firestore.rules:68 already caps text at 2000 chars, the title is clipped to 70 (mjs:67) and the inbox read is limited to 100 per run (mjs:21,58). inbox is create-only for clients (firestore.rules:57-60). What remains real: full-admin key exposed to floating transitive npm code and unpinned first-party actions, plus the unbounded sharedMerchants read. Template is not deployed, so fix-before-use, low.

### repo-exposure-5 - The frozen web app is still published with no security headers
- severity: low | verdict: confirmed | urgency: later
- where: `firebase.json:6-10 · index.html (no CSP meta) · CONTEXT.md:10 · FIREBASE.md:37`
- evidence: firebase.json hosting block has only public, ignore and a catch-all rewrite to /index.html: no headers section at all (no Content-Security-Policy, X-Frame-Options/frame-ancestors, Referrer-Policy, or cache rules). grep of index.html finds no CSP or frame directive. CONTEXT.md:10 says the project is published on the web.app address and FIREBASE.md:37 lists it and the firebaseapp.com domain as expected auth domains. The old app is frozen (no further fixes) but talks to the same Firebase project and the same users/{uid} data as the Kotlin app.
- impact: The old site can be framed by another page (clickjacking on a logged-in finance app) and has no script restrictions if any injection bug exists in code that will never be patched again. A public, unmaintained second front door to the same data.
- fix: If nobody uses the web app any more: run a hosting disable (owner approval needed for deploy actions) or deploy a static 'moved to the app' page. If it must stay: add a headers block with frame-ancestors 'none', a restrictive Content-Security-Policy, X-Content-Type-Options nosniff and Referrer-Policy.
- second reviewer: firebase.json:6-10 has only public, ignore and rewrites, no headers block; index.html has no CSP or frame directive; CONTEXT.md:10 and FIREBASE.md:37 name the web.app address. I could not verify from a read-only checkout that the site is still live, so that part rests on the docs. No innerHTML or dangerouslySetInnerHTML sinks found under src, so the missing CSP is defence in depth only, and clickjacking a personal finance app with a handful of users is a thin threat. Low is fair; the useful part of the finding is the owner decision to take the frozen site offline.
- OWNER DECISION NEEDED: Take the old web app offline, or keep it live and harden it.
